5.4
Dissipative Discussion
Experimentally we note that a state of maximum information over time may not re-
main so; therefore we have a temporal wear and tear of the information qualities of
the states of the system; this loss of information occurs due to operationally unavoid-
able perturbations, which we will denote with the generic term of dissipation.
We underline that the measures that are carried out in the states ω ∈Sa|τ with
τ > 0 are susceptible to dissipative phenomena8.
We have the following question:
If this dissipative phenomenon is quantifiable and therefore measurable9, then it
means that we can associate a physical quantity of the laboratory system with it,
since by definition physical quantities are such if and only if they can be measured in
our laboratory.
The problem that now arises is to determine this quantity and its properties.
Let’s see how it is possible tomanage this problem:
Given a finite partition P = {∆j}j∈I with I ⊂N of finite cardinality, we consider for
each τ ≥0 the probabilities:
pj(τ) = P(a ∈∆j, τ)ω ,
∀j ∈I
and we calculate the entropy of the measurement of a in the state ω ∈Sa at time
τ ∈R+:
H(ω, a, P, τ) = −
X
j∈I
pj(τ) log2(pj(τ))
We have the following experimental property:
8See section 1.2.
9In other words, if the experimenter notices that something has changed, it means that the
mutation is measurable; otherwise, how is it possible to notice the change?

H(ω, a, P, τ) ≥H(ω, a, P, 0) ,
∀τ ≥0
(5.12)

and not the entropy of the physical system.

Chapter 6
Mackey’s Model Revisited
The older generation almost always regards the younger as too mathematical.
Wightman 1969
As explained in the previous sections, we will assume that the measurements of
physical quantities are carried out in a laboratory linked to a reference system where
it is possible to establish when and where they occur. In this chapter we introduce
some fundamental properties that connect the sets of physical quantities with those of
their states in relation to the values of the measurements obtained. These properties
induced by laboratory experience can be related to mathematical axioms of the model
we are developing, axioms with all the physical-mathematical limits that we have well
described in the introductory chapter of this work.
We will divide the system of axioms into two groups: the first group called "static
axioms" concerns the definition of the expected value of a physical quantity in a
given state; the second group called "dynamic axioms" establishes what we should
understood by the temporal evolution of a physical system. In this chapter we will
only cover the static axioms of the system.
6.1
Static Axioms
In the model we adopted, a physical system of the laboratory is defined through the
following objects:
I.
A set X of observables, where the observables indicate all the physical quantities
which we can measure in our laboratory.
We emphasize that we do not make a choice on the set of observables which we
want to measure, but we take into account all observables1.

takes place (the way of preparing the experiment, monitoring procedure, etc.).

In summary, a physical system is described by a pair (X, S) where X is the set of the
observables and S is the set of states; to it there is associated a unique map (6.5)
with µω,a the associated measure to the observable a in the state ω of the laboratory
system3.
The uniqueness of the map (6.5) derives from the fact that the values assumed by the
observable a in the state ω (at a given fixed time) are uniquely established, experi-
mentally, by the relative frequencies fn (a ∈∆)ω.
Before proceeding with the discussion we must underline the following remarks:
• Single measurements - These considerations apply to single measurements
of the laboratory’s physical quantities. We repeat again that experimentally
no observable of the system can actually be prepared one at a time, since it is
always necessary to take into account other physical quantities that the experi-
menter knows and keeps under their rigid control; this information contributes
to establishing the state of the system ω. So when we talk about average values
of an observable we are referring to their individual measurements relative to
the ω state4.
• Unit of measure - Experimentally, a physical quantity has its own units of
measurement in which it is expressed. In this formalism we are assuming that
the values of measurements of physical quantities are dimensionless. In other
words we initially set particular reference values (fixed once and for all by our
experimenter) of the physical quantity we want to measure to which we will
compare the quantity itself.
For example for the electric charge Q we can
consider the dimensionless quantity bQ = Q/Qo where Qo is a test electric charge,
etc. This way of proceeding allows us to compare (and we will see later also
add) values of the measurements of some physical quantities not homogeneous
with each other.
For this purpose we give the following definitions5:
Definition 21. Let a and b be observables of our physical system; we will say that
the observable b extends a and we will write a ⊂b6, if

which has a profound meaning from the one assumed here.

• Sa ⊂Sb ,

In a symmetrical way we can give the definition for physical states:
Definition 22. Let ω and ω′ be states of our physical system; we will say that the
state ω′ extends ω and we will write ω ⊂ω′7, if
• Xω ⊂Xω′ ,
• ⟨a⟩ω = ⟨a⟩ω′ ,
∀a ∈Xω
In the axiomatic formalism we are outlining, the concepts of state and observable of
a physical system are primitive concepts having the following fundamental properties:
Axiom 4 (Identity of Observables). Two observables a and b of our physical
system are equal if and only if a ⊂b and b ⊂a.
For states we have:
Axiom 5 (Identity of States). Two states ω1 and ω2 of our physical system are
equal if and only if ω1 ⊂ω2 and ω2 ⊂ω1.
Remark 28. The axioms we have introduced are slightly different from those adopted
by Mackey in [84], since in addition to considering equality between average values
and not between probability measures, we consider a set of states of the system not
independent from the set of observables8.
Let’s make other considerations on the laboratory instruments designed to estab-
lish, through measurement, the value of physical quantities.
The measurement apparatus by its nature has a graduated scale, a counter where the
value of the measurement of our observables can be read, which in the Mackey scheme
is determined by the Borel measure µω,a.
What happens if we rescale (even in a non-linear way) our reading scale of the mea-
suring device?
In practice, we can say that a rescaling of the measurement apparatus is carried out
via a Borel function F : R →R; the values of the rescaled measurements are described
by the Borel measure F distribution law defined as follows:

6.1.1
Segal-von Neumann vs. Mackey II step

damental role in establishing equality between physical quantities and states of the
system.

malism, introduce weaker conditions between observables and states of the system,
compared to the relations between Borel measures adopted by Mackey, as highlighted
by the following observation:

Considering that the observables of the system are all bounded is not an important
restriction for our modeling.
Indeed by (6.22), we can use a bounded Borel function (set by our experimenter) to
"rescale" all measurements obtained.
Therefore it is not restrictive to assume the following fundamental phenomenological
principle (see Segal [115]):
The set of all bounded observables of a physical system defines the system completely,
in all its physically observable aspects.
In other words we assume:
Axiom 8 (Observable norm). For each observable a of the system we have
sup {| ⟨a⟩ω | : ω ∈Sa} < ∞
The value of the upper bound is usually called the norm of the observable a and is

Before proceeding, let us recall a well-known theorem that is a consequence of
Egorov’s theorem16.
Theorem 3 (Lusin). Let µ be a finite Borel measure on a compact metric space K.
For every µ-measurable function f : K →R and every ϵ > 0, there exists a compact
E ⊂K such that:
1. µ(K \ E) < ϵ
2. f|E is continuous.
Furthermore:
Corollary 1 (Lusin’s Theorem for Borel Functions). Let µ be a Borel measure
on R. For every Borel function f : R →R and compact subset K ⊂R, there exists
an equibounded net of continuous functions fα ∈C(K) that converges µ-a.e. to f on
K.17 Moreover, if f is bounded then we obtain
sup
x∈K
|f(x)| ≤∥fα∥∞
Proof. This is a consequence of Lusin’s theorem combined with the fact that conver-
gence in measure implies µ-a.e. convergence for some subsequence.

Mackey Spectrum
In Mackey’s work [84] we find the following definition of the spectrum of an observable
(see also [34]).
For every Borel set ∆of R, we consider the set of continuous functions:
F∆= {f ∈C(R) : 0 ≤f ≤1∆}
(6.35)
The Borel set ∆is said to be a-null according to Mackey if f(a) ⊂0 for all functions
f ∈F∆.
In [84] the resolvent of a is defined as the union of all open sets of R that are a-null
according to Mackey, and the spectrum is its complement.
Let us now ask ourselves whether our definition of spectrum coincides with Mackey’s.
To prove that this statement is true, it is sufficient to prove that the two definitions
of open a-null coincide.
If U is an open a-null then by definition it follows that µω,a(U) = 0 for every state ω
21The last inclusion follows from proposition 5.


## Page 134


120
CHAPTER 6. MACKEY’S MODEL REVISITED
and therefore from relation (6.15) we have 1U(a) ⊂0.
From relation (6.17) we obtain that for every f ∈FU
0 ≤⟨f(a)⟩ω ≤⟨1U(a)⟩ω = 0 ,
∀ω ∈Sa
therefore f(a) ⊂0 and the open set U is a-null according to Mackey.
Conversely, if the open set U of R is a-null according to Mackey then f(a) ⊂0 for
each f ∈FU and we consider any compact set K ⊂U.
By Urysohn’s lemma22, we can say that there exists a continuous function g with
compact support in U with 0 ≤g ≤1 and with g|K = 1.
It follows that g ∈FU and by hypothesis ⟨g(a)⟩ω = 0 for each ω suitable for a; this
implies that µω,a(K) = 0.
From the arbitrariness of the compact K ⊂U and from the previously discussed
regularity of the Borel measure µω,a, we obtain that µω,a(U) = 0 for all states ω
suitable for a and therefore U is an open a-null.

Value-States of an Observable
Let us now make some useful observations on the set of states of a physical system.
If λ ∈σ(a), then by the SPS property there exists at least one state ωo suitable for a
such that
⟨a⟩ωo = λ
The state ωo is called a value-state relative to the value λ of the observable a and the
set
Uλ(a) = {ω ∈Sa : ⟨a⟩ω = λ}
(6.51)

[

Let us now ask ourselves when the equality sign holds in the previous inclusion.
This means that for every state ω suitable for a, there exists a λ ∈σ(a) such that
⟨a⟩ω = λ
This property is called dual property of states.

Spectral Projectors of an Observable
Using axiom 6 we can construct question observables of our physical system. In fact
it is easy to verify that given an observable a of the system and a Borel set ∆of B(R),
the observable 1∆(a) is a question.
The question 1∆(a) is said to be the spectral projector relative to the observable a.
Furthermore, it is easy to verify the following double implication:
1∆1(a) < 1∆2 (a)
⇐⇒
µω,a(∆1) < µω,a(∆2)
∀ω ∈Sa
(6.55)
Can we say that every question observable is a spectral projector?

exists an observable a that satisfies the following properties:
1 - Sa = Sq,
2 - µω,a({0}) = P(q ∈{0})ω
,
∀ω ∈Sa.
It is useful to note that two observables a and b of X coincide if and only if
1∆(a) = 1∆(b)
for every Borel set ∆of B (R).
Indeed if this equality holds, then we also have Sa = Sb and so for every state ω
suitable for a and b we obtain
⟨1∆(a)⟩ω = ⟨1∆(b)⟩ω
therefore µω,a = µω,b and by axiom 4 we have that a = b.
Let’s now make some simple considerations about spectral projectors and the SPS

SPS property it follows that for every value s ∈[0, 1] there exists a state ωo ∈Sa such
that P(a ∈∆)ωo = s, since
⟨1∆(a)⟩ωo = P(a ∈∆)ωo
so if λ ∈σ(a) and the spectral projector 1{λ}(a) is non-trivial, then we can modulate
the state ω ∈Sa in such a way as to obtain
P(a ∈{λ})ω = 1

## Page 149


Chapter 7
Pure States and Borel Measures
In the previous sections we introduced the set of physical states of the system S
without providing it with any particular algebraic-topological structure, hence the
difficulty of producing mathematical tools to probe its properties. For example, we
cannot introduce the definition of a mixed state since we do not have a linear space
structure on this set; we will see that to do this we must exploit the properties of the
set of probability measures associated with the observables and states of the physical
system.
7.1
States and Measures
This section is a mathematical parenthesis; we will very briefly recall some simple
results and definitions of measure theory; for further discussion refer to Folland’s
book [52].
We consider a topological space X, locally compact and T2, and denote by B(X) the
family of its Borel sets.
We denote by Π(X)1 the set of probability measures defined on the Borel sets of X.
Let ν1 and ν2 be two σ-finite Borel measures on (X, B(X)) ; it is said that ν1 is
absolutely continuous with respect to ν2, in symbols ν1 ≪ν2, if we have ν1(∆) = 0
for every Borel set ∆for which ν2(∆) = 0.
If our measures are finite, by the Radon-Nikodym theorem, there exists a non-negative
function ρ ∈L1(X, ν2) such that
ν1(∆) =
Z
∆
ρ dν2 ,
∀∆∈B(X)
The function ρ is called the Radon-Nikodym derivative, in symbols:
dν1 = ρ dν2
1If there is no ambiguity about the use of the topological space X, we shall denote this set only
by the Greek letter Π.

Recall that two σ-finite measures ν1 and ν2 are disjoint, in symbols ν1 ⊥ν2, if
there exists a Borel set ∆such that ν1(∆) = 0 and ν2(R \ ∆) = 0.
As we have repeated several times, by the Riesz-Markov theorem, the set of prob-
ability measures Π are in one-to-one correspondence with the states of the C*-algebra
Co(X); we will identify these two sets by writing
Π = {µ ∈Co(X)∗: ∥µ∥= 1}
(7.2)
We denote by Πp the set of extremal points of Π, i.e. the set consisting of the pure
states (positive functionals of norm 1) of Co(X)∗.
It is well known that the set Πp is in one-to-one correspondence with the points of
the topological space X2:
Πp = {δr : r ∈X}
where δr is the Dirac measure associated with the point r ∈X.
Recall that the set Π is a W*-compact subset of Co(X)∗. Therefore by the Krein-
Milman theorem it is generated by its extremal points; this means that it coincides
with the intersection of all the convex sets of Co(R)∗that contain Πp.
Let us briefly recall the Lebesgue decomposition theorem for Borel probability
measures.

7.2
Convexity
Let ∆o be a Borel set of B (R); an observable a of X is said to be a certainty in ∆o
in the state ω of Sa if we have:
P (a ∈∆o)ω = 1
For example, a question observable is a certainty in the Borel set {0, 1} for each state
suitable for it.
We introduce the following notion of mixed state of the physical system:
5We highlight that here Q is in general different from {to}.


## Page 153


7.2. CONVEXITY
139
Definition 32 (Mixture). Let a be an observable of the system; a state ω of Sa is
a mixture of two states ω1 and ω2 of Sa in the measure of a, if there exists r ∈(0, 1)
such that
µω,a = (1 −r)µω1,a + rµω2,a
(7.9)
From this we easily obtain that
⟨a⟩ω = (1 −r) ⟨a⟩ω1 + r ⟨a⟩ω2
(7.10)
and by definition and by (7.9), for every F : R →R that is a-summable we obtain:
µω,F (a) = (1 −r)µω1,F (a) + rµω2,F (a)
(7.11)
We remark that if ω is a mixture of ω1 and ω2 in the measure of a and the observable
a is a certainty in ∆o in the ω state, then a still turns out to be a certainty in ∆o in
both the ω1 and ω2 states of the system.
In fact, let ∆o be a Borel set of R such that

The converse of this statement is not true:
If a is a certainty in some Borel set ∆o for the state ω1, then it is not necessarily still
so for the mixed state ω. In other words, mixtures can obscure certainties.

7.2.1
Sectors in the Measure of an Observable
In elementary quantum mechanics it is assumed that given two states of the system
suitable for the observable a it is always possible to determine an intermediate state
that allows us to measure a6:
Property 3 (Convexity). Let a be an observable of the system; if ω1 and ω2 are
states belonging to Sa, then for every real number r ∈[0, 1] there exists a state ω7 of
Sa such that
µω,a = (1 −r)µω1,a + rµω2,a
(7.15)
In practice this property says that it is always possible experimentally to re-arrange
the instruments/devices in the laboratory in order to obtain a state of the system in
which it is possible to modulate the measurement between the two values given by
the two starting states.
This statement is very strong and may not have any real experimental feasibility.
We will adopt a lighter version; we will assume that in the set M (a) there are
subsets in which the previous convexity property 3 holds, and precisely:
Axiom 10 (Measurement Sectors). The set M (a) has a family of convex subsets
{Mk (a)}k∈I of M(a) called the measurement sectors of the observable a, with the
following properties:

µωα,a ∈Mk(a) such that

Let X be a locally compact topological space and T2.

where

• πµ is the representation πµ : Cb(R) →B(Hµ) defined by

Recall that a representation π : A →B(H) of a C*-algebra with unit A is irreducible
if and only if its commutant in B(H) is π(A)′ = CI.
Furthermore, if φ is a state of A and (Hφ, πφ, Ωφ) is its GNS representation, then πφ
is irreducible if and only if φ is a pure state of A.
A simple application of the Radon-Nikodym theorem is the following theorem which
summarizes the relations between probability measures and representations9:
Proposition 18. Let µ and ν be elements of Π(X); we have:
• µ ≪ν if and only if πµ ≪πν ( πµ is a subrepresentation of πν);
9See Arveson’s book [13] §2, theorem 2.2.2.

7.7
Operational Vacuum
In this section we will try to define a particular state of the laboratory that possesses
an analog of the vacuum state in quantum field theory, namely the quantum state of
minimum energy of our physical system.
In our model we can define this particular state as follows:
The operational vacuum state is the state established by a specific procedure (and by
the experimental instruments that realize it) such that, for every physical quantity
that this procedure allows us to measure, its value is minimal with respect to any
other experimental procedure (i.e., with respect to any other physical state).
In our symbols:
Definition 39. The state ωo ∈S is an operational vacuum if for every x ∈Xωo we
have:
⟨x⟩ωo ≤⟨x⟩ω
∀ω ∈Sx
Heuristically, we can think of this state as "white noise", a reference state defined
by measurement procedures, instruments, and the operator.
Recall that in an experimental procedure, white background noise is:
- The signal measured when no source is active;
- It depends on the instrumentation;
- It is subtracted to reveal the signal;
- It is an operational convention and different laboratories have different back-
ground noises.
Thus, in the preparation of this state, the role of the operator performing the
measurement is fundamental, since:
- they choose the sensitivity of the instruments (detection thresholds);
- they choose the calibration procedure (background subtraction);
- they define the threshold between "background" and "signal".


## Page 174


160
CHAPTER 7. PURE STATES AND BOREL MEASURES
However, once the experimental procedures are fixed (choice of instrumentation, set-
tings, calibration, detection thresholds), the actual execution of the measurements
can be carried out by any operator (or even by an automatic device) who strictly
follows the established instructions. In this sense, during the execution phase, the
operator is a cold executor, interchangeable, and plays no active role in modifying the
measurement outcome. Nevertheless, the definition of the operational vacuum state
depends on the initial choices made by the operator (or the research team) before the
experiment begins.
We can assign a numerical value to the background noise through the variance of
an observable, previously defined, namely the value
Rf(ωo) := inf {∆ωo(a) : a ∈Xωo}
It should be emphasized that the existence of such states in our model is not
guaranteed a priori; it must be postulated:
Postulate 11 (Existence and Positive Background Noise). The laboratory system
defined by the pair (X, S) admits at least one operational vacuum state.
For every operational vacuum state ωo ∈S we have
Rf(ωo) > 0
In other words, no physical quantity accessible through the procedure that defines
ωo can be measured with zero variance in this state; that is, ∆ωo(a) > 0. This implies
that the state ωo might not be a pure state with respect to the measurement of a.

Chapter 8
Dynamic Axioms
Building upon the static axioms introduced earlier, which remain largely consistent
with Mackey’s foundational work [83, 84], we now turn to the investigation initiated
in Section 5.3 concerning the temporal evolution of physical quantities. This section
establishes a new set of principles governing these dynamical changes, which we shall
refer to as the dynamic axioms.
8.1

8.3
Evolution and Dissipation
In this section, we resume the discussion initiated in Section 5.4. Empirical obser-
vations demonstrate that dissipative processes typically transform pure states into
mixed states, while the reverse transition from mixed states to pure states does not
generally occur. That is, states that become mixed through temporal evolution remain
mixed indefinitely. To formalize this behavior, we introduce the following fundamental
property:
Definition 40. The temporal evolution (8.7) is said to possess the affine property if
it satisfies the following condition:
For any mixed state ω at initial time τo = 0 that is a convex combination of states
ω1, ω2 ∈Sa in the measurement of observable a:
µω,a = rµω1,a + (1 −r)µω2,a

Da(τ) := Ext(Sa|τ=0) ∩Ext(Sa|τ) ,
τ ≥0

at initial time τ = 0
• Ext(Sa|τ) represents the pure states in the measurement of a at evolution time
τ > 0

Definition 42. A physical system (X, S) exhibits:
1. Reversible (non-dissipative) evolution up to time T > 0 if it preserves
pure states for all observables:
Ext(Sa|τ) = Ext(Sa|τ=0),
∀a ∈X ,
∀τ ∈[0, T]
2. Irreversible (dissipative) evolution if there exists an a ∈X and a τ∗> 0
such that
Ext(Sa|τ∗) ⊊Ext(Sa|τ=0)
3. Completely irreversible evolution if for every a ∈X there exists a τ∗> 0
such that
Ext(Sa|τ∗) ∩Ext(Sa|τ=0) = ∅

## Page 187


Chapter 9
Compatible Observables
As we have highlighted, for experimental reasons it is often necessary to perform si-
multaneous measurements of two or more observables; the meaning of this statement
has been widely discussed in the previous sections. Here we want to resume the dis-
cussion to establish the meaning of the sum and product of observables performed in
the same state ω at an instant of time τ.
The simultaneity of the measurement of some observables of the physical system al-
lows us to introduce particular algebraic operations of sum and product in the set
of observables. The first attempts to give a valid algebraic structure associated to a
quantum system are due to various works by von Neumann and Jordan1 and subse-
quently by Segal in [114].
For a historical and epistemological discussion on the algebraization of quantum
physics, the interested reader can certainly consult the books by Primas [102] and
by Emch [47].
9.1
Function of an Observable and Compatibility
Let’s consider a generic observable a of our physical system; we have seen in the
previous sections what we mean by F(a) where F : R →R is a generic Borel function.
Precisely, it is an observable such that for every Borel set ∆of R we have:
P(F(a) ∈∆)ω = P(a ∈F −1(∆))ω ,
∀ω ∈Sa
(9.1)

ω suitable for a, since by measuring a we can know through (9.1) the value of F(a)
(and vice versa), through the equality
P(F(a) ∈∆0 : a ∈∆1)ω = P(a ∈F −1(∆0) ∩∆1)ω

9.2
Sum and Product of Compatible Observables
The real number sum of two observables is not always a value that can be related to
an observable that is actually measurable.
Intuitively, compatible observables, since they are not influenced by the mutual prepa-
rations that we can do on them4 can be added, so the sum of the expected values of
two compatible observables can be derived from an expected value of a new observable
of our physical system. This statement introduces a new axiom:
4Preparing them individually or jointly does not change their probability measure µω,a.

Proof. It’s trivial.
As in the case of the sum, given any two observables a and b of the physical
system and individually establishing their distribution laws at the time τ (marked
by the clock in our laboratory), P(a ∈∆, τ)ω and P(b ∈∆, τ)ω, one could think of
carrying out the numerical multiplication of the various experimentally determined
values of the two observables to establish the numerical value of the product of the
two observables in question. This way of operating is experimentally incorrect, since
the numerical value established in this way does not always actually correspond to an
observable of the physical system. We can give a physical (operational) sense only to
the product of compatible observables; to do this we use the sum and square operation
of an observable established in the previous sections7.
We want to recall that if a and b are compatible observables, then f(a) and g(b) for
every f, g ∈B∞(R) are compatible observables.
Jordan Product

The next step in the mathematical modelling of observables is to determine par-
ticular families of observables that possess a well-defined algebraic structure, to which
end the next section is devoted.
9.3
Notes on Jordan Algebras*
Let us now briefly recall the theory of Jordan algebras8.
Definition 44. [Jordan Algebra] A real Jordan algebra B is a real linear space
with a product, called the Jordan product, which satisfies the following properties:
For every A, B, C ∈B
• Commutativity: A ◦B = B ◦A
• Distributivity: A ◦(B + C) = A ◦B + A ◦C
• Weak Associativity: A2 ◦(B ◦A) = (A2 ◦B) ◦A
Following Jordan et al. (see Jordan [69] p. 32), the algebra B is formally real if
A2 + B2 = 0
=⇒
A = B = 0
Let B1 and B2 be two Jordan algebras; a R-linear map Φ : B1 →B2 is a Jordan
morphism if it preserves the Jordan product:
Φ(A ◦B) = Φ(A) ◦Φ(B) ,
∀A, B ∈B1
We denote by L(B) the set of linear operators from B to itself and for each A ∈B we
define the multiplication operator:
TAX = A ◦X ,
∀X ∈B
(9.12)
We note that TATB = TBTA if and only if
B ◦(X ◦A) = (B ◦X) ◦A ,
∀X ∈B
(9.13)
For every A ∈B we define the set
Z(A) = {B ∈B : TATB = TBTA}
8For more details see the text by Hanche-Olsen and Størmer [58].


## Page 195


9.3. NOTES ON JORDAN ALGEBRAS*
181
while the center of the Jordan algebra is given by the set:
Z(B) =
\
A∈B
Z(A) ⊂B
(9.14)
The linear subspace Z(B) of B is an associative algebra (see [58] Lemma 2.5.3):
1. x ◦y ∈Z(B) , ∀x, y ∈Z(B) ;
2. x ◦(y ◦z) = (x ◦y) ◦z , ∀x, y, z ∈Z(B) .

A ◦B = 1

A ◦B = 1

standard notation is used:
[A, B] = AB −BA
,

which are called the commutator and the anti-commutator of the algebra, respectively.
Furthermore, if we consider the product
A ∗B = 1

AB = A ◦B + A ∗B ,
∀A, B ∈A
(9.16)

the Jordan algebras and the associative algebra.

Definition 46. [Commuting Relative] Let D be any subset of the real associative
algebra A ; we define its commuting relative in A as the set11
Dc = {A ∈A : AX = XA ∀X ∈D} ⊂A
It is easily verified that the elements of Dc constitute an abelian subalgebra of the
associative algebra A.
Let us take into consideration the Jordan algebra A(+) induced by the real associative

Ac := {A ∈A : AB = BA , ∀B ∈A} ⊂Z(A(+))

Remark 54. Let A be a subalgebra of B(HR) of bounded operators on a real Hilbert
space HR. We have
Z(A) := A′ ∩A = Ac
where A′ denotes the commutant of the algebra A in B(HR):
A′ = {T ∈B(HR) : AT = TA , ∀A ∈A}
Thus we obtain:
Z(A) ⊂Z(A(+))
In other words, this remark tells us that the center Z(A) of the subalgebra of
bounded operators on a Hilbert space A is included in the center Z(A(+)) of the Jor-
dan algebra A(+), but we cannot say that it coincides12

In the following we will consider real Jordan algebras contained in the set of self-

In this regard, please note that14 a Jordan algebra JC [JW ] is a Jordan algebra
B contained in B(H)s.a., closed in norm [weakly closed] with the product given by
equation (9.15).
Obviously a JC algebra is a JB algebra.
We have the following statement15:
Proposition 29. [Topping] Let A, B ∈B(H)s.a.; we have:
AB = BA
⇐⇒
TATB = TBTA
with
TAX = A ◦X = 1
2(AX + XA) ,

Therefore, if B ⊂B(H)s.a. is a Jordan JC algebra, then we have16
Z(B) = {A ∈B : AB = BA , ∀B ∈B} = B ∩B′ = Z(B)
(9.19)
Warning 10. Let us remember that a JB-algebra generally cannot be represented as
an algebra of operators on a Hilbert space, and therefore for these algebras it makes
no sense to talk about closure in the weak topology [117].
The analogue of real W*-algebras in Jordan algebras is given by JBW-algebras:
A real JBW-algebra is a Jordan algebra isomorphic to the dual of a real Banach vector
space.
We underline that a Jordan JW-algebra is also a Jordan JBW-algebra.
We have the following result which is found in [117] Theorem 3.917:
Theorem 7. [Alfsen-Shultz] A JBW-Jordan algebra B admits a unique direct sum
decomposition:
B = Bsp ⊕Bex
where Bsp is isomorphic to a Jordan JW-algebra, while Bex is purely exceptional18.

Every experimental apparatus exhibits an intrinsic resolution limit δ, such that for
any physical observable a:
• Measurements cannot distinguish values within intervals smaller than δ.
• The spectral projection E[t−δ/2,t+δ/2] represents the minimal detectable event,
where
E∆:= 1∆(a)
for each Borel set ∆⊂R.
• Expectation values ⟨f(a)⟩ω are empirically indistinguishable from coarse-grained
averages:

where {tk}k forms a δ-spaced grid of σ(a)1.
This means that we can discretize the observable’s spectrum into small intervals
[tk −δ/2, tk + δ/2], where f is nearly constant.
In this chapter, we will analyze the meaning of such statements mathematically.5

Physical Interpretation
In summary, the spectral decomposition theorem establishes two operating principles:
• Measurement Granularity:
The partition {∆k}n
k=1 corresponds to detector resolution limits in experiments
(through the value of ϵ > 0).
• Statistical Convergence:
The state convergence (10.2) reflects how finite-precision measurements ap-
proach ideal expectations.

Chapter 11
Function of Several Observables
In this section we introduce the functional calculus for simultaneously preparable
and compatible observables . We will define a particular subset of Rn of all possible
values that a family of n jointly preparable observables in a given order can assume
simultaneously, which will be called the joint spectrum.
11.1
Joint Spectrum
Let us now resume the study carried out in section 3.3. Let a and b be two non-
complementary (not necessarily compatible) observables that we want to measure
simultaneously; furthermore we assume that the preparation is a : b (a prepared
before b). As we have seen in equation (3.7), page 59, there is a probability measure,
which we have denoted by µω,a:b ∈Π(R2), such that:
P(a ∈∆1 : b ∈∆2)ω = µω,a:b(∆1 × ∆2) ,
∆1, ∆2 ∈B(R)
(11.1)
As we have already said, we treat the pair a : b as a single observable of the two-valued
system:
P(a : b ∈∆1 × ∆2)ω = µω,a:b(∆1 × ∆2) ,
ω ∈Sa:b
We can regard a : b as an element of the S-simultaneous Cartesian product (see
Figure 11.1), defined as follows:
X ×S X := {(a, b) ∈X × X : a is jointly preparable with b in the order a : b}
Clearly, the compatible observables are positioned symmetrically with respect to the
bisector axis of X × X1.
1This set will consist of a discrete collection of points, with
X ×S X ⊂X × X

11.1. JOINT SPECTRUM

Adjoint of Jointly Preparable Observables
Let a, b be non-complementary observables of the system with the property that it is
possible to prepare a before b and b before a for the experiment, and such that
Sa:b = Sb:a
These observables are not necessarily compatible, but only jointly preparable — i.e.,
preparable in both orders — and this class of observables may be denoted as non-
orthogonal observables of the system2.
We emphasize that if a, b are non-orthogonal, the observables may be simultaneously
measurable independently but not compatible according to our definitions, because
the associated measures µω,a:b and µω,b:a may differ, and the observable a : b need
not coincide with b : a. For this class of observables, we define a ∗operation of time
reversal (cf. Accardi [8]):
(a : b)∗= b : a
These considerations extend straightforwardly to a family of non-orthogonal observ-
ables of the system.
Joint Spectrum and Functional Calculus
We introduce a new property that generalizes axiom 6 of the functional calculus:
Axiom 15. For each pair of observables a, b jointly preparable in the order a : b and
for every bounded Borel function F : R2 →R, we have an observable of the laboratory,
which we will denote by F(a : b), such that
SF (a:b) = Sa:b
(11.2)
and
µω,F (a:b)(∆) = µω,a:b(F −1(∆)) ,

(11.3)

P(F(a : b) ∈∆)ω := P(a : b ∈F −1(∆))ω = µω,a:b(F −1(∆))

and so
σ(a : a) = σ(a) × σ(a)
11.2
Functional Calculus and Compatibility
Let us return to the study of the main properties of the functional calculus.
Axiom 16. Given a pair of observables a, b jointly preparable in the order a : b, for
each pair of bounded Borel functions f, g : R →R we have:
• the observables f(a) and g(b) are jointly preparable in the same order:
a : b =⇒f(a) : g(b)
• they have the same joint states:
Sa:b = Sf(a):g(b)
4Formally, because the simultaneous measurement of a and a itself (we are not making a repeated
measurement of a) does not, of course, make physical sense.

## Page 231


Chapter 12
Selection of States and
Observables
As argued in detail in the previous sections, to our laboratory system, located in O,
we have associated a pair (X, S) consisting of all the physical quantities that it is
potentially possible to measure in the laboratory in a given preparation time and all
the possible states in which these measurements are made. However, in the work done
so far we have not taken the following fact into account:
It makes no physical sense to take into account all the various devices and measuring
equipment of the laboratory system, because we can only use a finite family of them
(or at most a countable limit) and thus only really make measurements on a finite
family of observables (or at most a countable limit) So of S.
In this section we will highlight the minimal properties which this family of states
(and observables) must satisfy to determine a physical subsystem of the system.
12.1
Physical Laboratory Systems
Let’s make a direct choice on the states of the physical system (X, S) of our labo-
ratory, considering (for example for experimental reasons) a subset So ⊂S and we
study the consequences of this selection.
The first question we ask ourselves is the following:
What subset Xo ⊂X can be associated with the state set So?
An initial answer to this question was given in section 1.4 on page 28:
To make physical sense, the pair (Xo, So) must necessarily satisfy at least the physi-
cally achievable conditions:
1. For every observable a belonging to Xo, there must be at least one state ω ∈So
suitable for it, so:
Xo ⊂XSo
∨
=
[

12.1.1
Relative Spectrum
We observed in section 1.3 that fixing a set of states So of the system influences the set
of possible observables, since a state of the system is synonymous with an experimental
procedure, a procedure implemented with instruments and devices present in the
laboratory2. This led us to the notion of a physically achievable pair (Xo, So).
One effect of this choice is to have the spectrum of an observable a ∈Xo smaller than
the totality of the states, in symbols3:
σSo(a) ⊂σ(a)

ing) the complexity of the measurements that can be carried out on the observables
of the system, thereby increasing (or decreasing) their knowledge (for example, their
spectrum becomes larger or smaller) and even limiting what is physically impossible
to know about the observables themselves4:

12.1.3
Sum of So-compatible Observables
Let (Xo, So) be an achievable pair, and consider two observables x, y of Xo which are
So-compatible. What can we say about their sum?
In general, we cannot affirm that there exists the sum of x and y such that
⟨x + y⟩ω = ⟨x⟩ω + ⟨y⟩ω ,
∀ω ∈So
(12.3)
and even if that observable exists, it does not necessarily belong to the set Xo and is
So-compatible with x and y.
To solve this problem, we introduce a generalization of property 5 in the case of
So-joint preparation (see Axiom 15 on page 197):

12.3
Restricted States and Observables
In section 6.1 on page 105, we introduced the axioms governing the relation that exists
between states and observables and their average values obtained through measure-
ments made using the ensemble procedure.
In this section we introduce additional properties13 that link the set of observables
with the set of states of a physical system, properties that are in line with experimental
procedures carried out in the laboratory.
Property 8. [Restricted State]
Let ω ∈S and Xo ⊂Xω. A state ωo ∈S14 is
called a restricted state of ω to the set Xo if it satisfies the following properties:
1. ωo ⊂ω;
2. Xo = Xωo.
The restriction ωo of ω to Xo is denoted by ω|Xo
15.
This property affirms that the instruments, and thus the related procedures in-
cluded in ω, are limited in their use, being directed only to the measurement of the
observables in the family Xo.
In particular, if the set Xo consists only of the observable a, then we have a restricted
state of ω suitable only for its measurement16.
We denote by S⊙the set of states which admit the restricted state ω|Xo; it necessarily
follows that
S⊙⊂SXo
∧
and we may consider the non-empty set
So = {ω|Xo : ω ∈S⊙} ⊂SXo
∧
Therefore, the pair (Xo, So) is a suitable system, but not necessarily a physical sub-
system.
Similarly, we introduce the following property concerning observables.
Property 9. [Restricted Observable]
Let a ∈X and So ⊂Sa. An observable
ao ∈X is called a restricted observable of a to the set So if it satisfies the following
properties:
1. ao ⊂a;
2. So = Sao.

CHAPTER 12. SELECTION OF STATES AND OBSERVABLES
12.6
Classical and Quantum Systems
As we have pointed out, the compatibility of two or more observables depends on the
set of states that we use to make our measurements, a set fixed by the experimenter
through the measuring instruments that he uses to prepare and measure physical
quantities. Increasing the possible states of the system means increasing the com-
plexity of the experiment, of the measurements that can be made on the observables
of the system, thus increasing our knowledge of them. Therefore, the center of the
observables of the system defined by Definition 48 on page 184 depends on the set of
states that we take into consideration.
Let us reiterate the concept: increasing the number of measuring instruments and
their preparations essentially means increasing the possibility of determining a state
for which two observables can be complementary.

12.7
Inferences
Paraphrasing the definition in the encyclopaedia [126], inference can be considered as
a generalization of the results obtained through a partial sample survey, i.e., limited
to the consideration of a few individual cases of the phenomenon under study, to the
totality of the cases of the phenomenon itself, on the basis of plausible hypotheses26.
In this section we will briefly study this notion by applying it to our interpretative
model of values obtained through experiments.

## Page 251


Part III
Measurement Procedures in
More Laboratories
237


## Page 253


Chapter 13
Reference and States
In this chapter we resume the study of the physical laboratory system from the op-
erational point of view, addressing the various problems avoided in section 1.3 of the
initial chapter.
Let’s start the discussion by remembering that in our case to observe is synonymous
with to measure, and to measure you must have instruments at hand. It makes no
experimental sense to say that we observe a physical phenomenon in a given reference
system without saying how to carry it out; to do so, you need to have a laboratory
available to carry out measurements, equipped at least with rulers for distances and
clocks for time. In this way, a reference system is a real measurement apparatus to be
positioned at a chosen point O of the laboratory; without it we cannot establish the
physical state of the system, hence its role as the primary instrument of the laboratory
follows.
13.1
Clocks, Rulers and States
In this section we will highlight what role the adopted reference system plays in the
measurement process of physical quantities. We assume that the laboratory has a
spatial extension given by a bounded, open and connected set Lo of a Euclidean
topological space E1.
In agreement with Einstein (see [45]), we assume that at every point of the laboratory
it is possible to associate clocks that are all synchronized with a clock located at a
well-fixed point O of the laboratory that the experimenter uses to establish when
events happen, and we assume that
Postulate 12. All clocks positioned anywhere in the laboratory, once synchronized,
keep the same time as our reference clock positioned at O.
We note that this postulate is not always true; for example, clocks run more or
less slowly in the presence of gravity, which must be taken into account if we consider
1In general it may turn out not to be simply connected.
239


## Page 254


240
CHAPTER 13. REFERENCE AND STATES
very large laboratories or have instruments with a large mass2.
From this hypothesis, it follows that every instrument in the laboratory must be sub-
ject to the same acceleration (which can even be zero) and this excludes the possibility
of there being laboratory devices in relative motion with each other.
Let’s make a further clarification on this last statement:
A measuring instrument occupies a more or less large space in the laboratory; inside it
we can have moving mechanisms that allow the instrument to carry out its measuring
function. What we are stating is that the reading of the value of the measurement
given by the instrument at a given time τ is carried out with a clock that satisfies our
postulate 12.
For example, let us assume we have a geostationary satellite with various instru-
ments and an operations centre at an Earth base where we place our O clock. One
might think of extending the Earth laboratory to include the satellite and the base
itself. In our definition of a laboratory, this cannot happen, since, by definition, if
we place a clock anywhere in the laboratory, as established by postulate 12, it must
always mark the same time as indicated by the clock at O. In this case, the satellite is
moving and therefore time flows differently from our reference clock, and the reading
of the measurements taken by the satellite is carried out in the satellite (and then
possibly communicated to the ground base)3.
So far we have only used a clock fixed at a point O in space, thus obtaining the
laboratory-type region:
Oo = Lo × [0, tp] ⊂E × R
(13.1)
But when do spatial coordinates come into play?
In order to establish the state of the laboratory system, the experimenter must
have the ability to say where the various instruments are operating and when they
are activated. For this purpose we establish a methodology to label the points of
the laboratory environment space; to do this we will use oriented rulers4, which we
will denote by K = (e1, e2, e3), and thus obtain spatial coordinates with origin at O,
where our reference clock resides. We denote this space-time reference by (K, O)5.
The question we ask ourselves now is the following: Does the state of the system de-
pend on the choice of the spatial reference K that we have taken into consideration?
Physically, in establishing the state ω of the system in the measurement of its ob-
servable a, what is important is the position, and hence the mutual position, of the
2In general we must also ask ourselves the question of whether the graduated scales of the various
instruments depend on their location in the laboratory.
3We will see that what we can do is to consider two distinct laboratories: the satellite laboratory
and the ground-base laboratory.
4If desired, we can also use rulers and goniometers, but it is always better to limit the use of other
types of instruments with their associated physical quantities, such as angles.
5In practice, with K we also indicate the three numbers that are used to identify the points
in space with respect to the point O, plus the time value of the laboratory indicated by the clock
positioned at O.


## Page 255


13.1. CLOCKS, RULERS AND STATES
241
various devices in the laboratory, which would seem to be independent of the spatial
coordinates we adopt to indicate them. Is this sufficient to affirm that the state does
not depend on the choice of the adopted coordinate reference (K, O)?
The answer is no, because clocks and rulers are themselves devices that the experi-
menter uses in the laboratory, and changing their position could change the state of
the system6.
Remark 70. The chosen point O must be taken inside the laboratory Lo, because all
measuring instruments/equipment must be in the laboratory, and placing the origin
of the reference system outside the laboratory would mean changing the very extent
of the laboratory.
In summary, we imagined positioning our experimenter at a point O of Lo ⊂E
and fixing a reference system K centered at O to identify the relative position of the
instruments/devices for measurement.
In other words, at O we have positioned our operations center (which we have briefly
denoted by the name of operator, experimenter, observer, etc.) which prepares, acti-
vates the various devices present in the laboratory, records and analyzes and transmits
their data.
In this way, once the state of the system has been established, we can consider the
Lo laboratory as a single measuring instrument, an instrument equally spread across
the entire Lo region which detects the value of our observable to be measured in our
state of the system at a given time τ established by our clock positioned at O.
Figure 13.1: R.S. change and preparation time

13.2. TIME OBSERVABLE

Figure 13.2: Source
Unlike the measuring devices/equipment D, which must reside in the laboratory Lo,
the source S may not. For example, if we want to determine the velocity or position of
an object S moving in space, as depicted in Figure 13.2, we have that S is intercepted
by the instruments of Lo at time τ, identified through a reference system.
We reiterate the concept with the following
Remark 72. When we speak of observables of the laboratory system Lo located
in the laboratory-type region Oo, we mean that the measurement of the physical
quantity takes place in this region of space-time, in a precise reference system8, even
if the source S, the object of the measurement, is not necessarily contained in it, as
in the case of Figure 13.2.
13.2
Time Observable
Let’s start by underlining that we have taken the clock as a real laboratory measuring
instrument9.
Time is a fundamental unit in many systems of units of measurement, and in the
International System the definition of standard time is well established thanks to the
advent of atomic clocks10. It follows that time has the same dignity as a physical
quantity as length and mass; therefore, as such, it is an observable of the physical
laboratory system.
For the time observable t of our laboratory Lo, we can detect the following features:
8Information on the reference system adopted is contained in the ω state of the system we have
chosen for our measurements.
9See also Fabri’s book [50] - section: gli orologi come strumenti fisici.
10See Muga et al. [88], Chap. 1.


## Page 258


244
CHAPTER 13. REFERENCE AND STATES
• It needs no preparation time, as the clock is already present and functioning,
and it is the first instrument to be activated in the laboratory.
• The set of its suitable states St(Oo) is made up of the various types of clocks
and the way they are used, etc.
• It is prepared before each observable a of the system, so it is jointly prepared
in the order t : a, since a is measured at a time τ:
St:a(Oo) ⊂Sa(Oo)
,
∀a ∈X
Although it sounds like a play on words, we must be careful not to confuse the
time observable t with its value tO marked by the clock positioned in our laboratory
at O ∈Lo11.
We note that for each state ω ∈St(Oo) which measures the time indicated by our
laboratory clock located at O ∈Lo, we have:
P(t ∈{τ} : tO)ω = 1
⇐⇒
τ = tO
We observe that this statement is tautological because the clock is the first measuring
instrument that our experimenter makes operational in his laboratory.
For the spectrum of the observable t the following property is assumed12:
Postulate 13 (Continuous Time).
σ(t) = R
Therefore its spectral measure is given by
µt
ω,t = δt ,
∀t ≥0
In summary, the time observable t is jointly preparable with every observable a of
the system in the order t : a, but it is not compatible with any observable of the
laboratory system13 since it must necessarily be prepared before each observable.
This last statement could be a methodological solution to Pauli’s old problem of time
as a parameter 14.
To summarize, we have redefined the concept of a reference system as a measure-
ment apparatus located in the laboratory. It follows that:
- Time is not an external parameter, but an observable t with a continuous spec-
trum.
11We will often forget about the clock at O and denote tO simply as t.
12We have already discussed that for measurements it would be physically more appropriate to

that one can take arbitrarily small time intervals is a strong assumption.
13According to our definition given in Definition 12.

13.3. INCLUSION PROBLEMS
245
- Clock synchronization is a hypothesis, not a mathematical axiom.
- A change of reference transforms the state of the system Θ, not just the coor-
dinates.
- The source may lie outside the laboratory, but the measurement takes place
inside.
13.3
Inclusion Problems
In the previous sections we established that each laboratory region Lo ⊂E is associ-
ated with a set of states S(Lo) which describes all the possible equipment/instruments
and their procedures/modes of use, etc., that can be implemented in Lo.
We also pointed out that in the real case, not all the devices will be available to those
who carry out experiments in the laboratory; however, this experimenter must always
have a clock available, positioned somewhere in the laboratory for time, and some
oriented rulers to establish the distance and position of each individual device in the
laboratory.
We have noticed that changing the reference system leads to a reshuffling of the set of
states through the map given in (13.3); for example, if we establish that the state of
the system with respect to (K, O) is given by ω, then changing the laboratory system
from (K, O) to (K′, O′), the state will change to Θ(ω):

As we have repeatedly reiterated, the experimenter decides, based on his objec-
tives, which states to choose in the set S(L1). For example, even if he has extended
his laboratory, he could decide to use only that part of the instruments located in Lo.
But how does this selection happen starting from S(L1)?
One method to achieve this selection is to choose a set of states S• of S(L1) given
by16:
S• = {ω ∈S(L1) : Xω(L1) ⊂X(Lo)} ⊂S(L1)
(13.6)
Let us now ask ourselves another question:
Using the set of states S•, can we obtain all the observables of X(Lo)?
The answer to this question is affirmative if the following statement is assumed to be
true:
Property 10. Given a ∈X(Lo), for every ω ∈Sa(Lo) there exists a state (not
necessarily unique) bω ∈S• suitable for a17, such that
µω,a = µbω,a
We now assume property 10 to be true and fix a laboratory reference system
(K, O) for Lo; in this way it also becomes a reference for L1, since O ∈Lo ⊂L1.
Let a ∈X(Lo) and ω ∈S(Lo). Using this reference system, we relate a state ω1 ∈
S• ∩Sa(L1).
Now if we change the system from (K, O) to (K′, O′) with O′ ∈L1 as shown in Figure
13.3, the reference system of the laboratory is only L1.
The state ω1 ∈S(L1) previously identified will have mutated into the new state
Θ(ω1) ∈S(L1).
We observe that in this case we cannot say that Θ(ω1) satisfies relation (13.6) and is
therefore still a state of S•.
In the next sections we will study the problems related to the inclusion of laboratory
Lo with its own reference system into that of L1.

Before concluding this short section on changing coordinates, it is useful to note
the following facts.
We introduced the choice of the group of transformations G in a purely mathemati-
cal way, independent of the experimental act. In reality, to determine a coordinate
transformation (13.7), experimental results must be taken into account. For exam-
ple, consider the study of the decay time of an elementary particle in two distinct
laboratories LO and LO′ not at rest with respect to each other. As is known20, if
this elementary particle is at rest with respect to the laboratory LO′ with reference
system (K′, O′) centered at O′, then its decay time, measured with the instruments
and procedures of laboratory LO21, is dilated.
This leads us to consider the Poincaré group as the transformation group G22, and if
the velocity and acceleration of O′ relative to O are small (compared to the speed of
light), these values of the decay time differ slightly; in this case we can assume the
Galilean group as the group of transformations.

Symmetries in the Laboratory
Recall that given a reference system (K, O) of the laboratory Lo, we can write Lo ⊂

states that can be measured and prepared in this laboratory, respectively.
From Roberts and Roepstorff [108] we have:
A symmetry of a physical system is intuitively a transformation of the system leaving
all physically significant features invariant.

Remark 74. If x ∈C(a), then it does not necessarily follow that α(x) ∈C(α(a)),
where C(a) is the set of observables compatible with a.
As we discussed in section 1.1, observables are physical quantities that remain so
in time and space; what changes are their values, but not their typology. To have a
broader mathematical definition, in the definition of symmetry we considered a map
α that transforms the observables of the system, since the symmetries of a physical
system could be induced by processes internal to the system itself, independent of
space-time23.
13.5.1
Operations
A group G is called a group of operations on the physical system of the laboratory
(X(Lo), S(Lo)) if G acts on the set S(Lo); in other words, if for every g belonging to
G there is a map γg : S(Lo) −→S(Lo) such that for each g and h in G we have
γgh = γg ◦γh
The map γ is called an operation on the physical system.
We now have a set of definitions.
Let H be a subgroup of the operation group G. A set So ⊂S is said to be H-stable
if
γh(So) ⊂So ,
∀h ∈H
and a state ωo is G-stable if
γg(ωo) = ωo ,
∀g ∈G
while an observable a of the laboratory system is said to be G-invariant if for every
g ∈G we have
γg(Sa) ⊂Sa
23By internal symmetries one usually means, for elementary particles, symmetries that are inde-
pendent of the space-time structure of the world (see Nuyts in [24]).

⟨a⟩ω = ⟨a⟩γg(ω) ,

what connection exists between the generators of its Lie algebra and the G-invariant
observables of the system?
The group G of operations on the physical system is said to be symmetric if the
operations leave the behaviour of the system unchanged. In mathematical terms, this
means that there is a group homomorphism
g ∈G −→(id, γg) ∈Sym(Lo)
(13.12)

γg(Sa) ⊂Sa
and
µa,ω = µa,γg(ω) ,
∀ω ∈Sa(Lo)

Chapter 14
Reference and Laboratory
Inclusions
In this section we will address the issue of sublaboratories of our physical laboratory
and its division into two or more parts. We observe that in circumscribing a part of
our laboratory, a particular selection of states (and thus observables) is made, which
falls under the arguments covered in section 12.1 of our mother laboratory.
In addition, we will consider two separate laboratories at rest with respect to each
other and study their mutual independence.
We emphasize that these problems find their proper territory in the general framework
given by the theory of relativity, a topic that we will analyze only briefly in this section.

14.1.1
Localized Observables
There are observables of the laboratory system such that even by increasing the size
of the laboratory and the preparation times of the various procedures, we never obtain
an increase in information about the observable itself. This fact suggests introducing
the following definition of geometric localization of an observable7:
Definition 63. [Localized Observable] An observable a ∈X(Oo) is said to be
geometrically localized in Oo if for every laboratory-type region O1 containing Oo it
holds that8:
Sa(O1|Oo) = Sa(O1)
Before introducing the notion of a global observable, we must make some simple
observations.
If a ∈X(Oo), then for every pair of laboratory-type regions O1 and O2 with Oo ⊂
O1 ⊂O2 it turns out that:
a ∈X(Oo) ⊂X(O1) ⊂X(O2)
with
Sa(O1|Oo) ⊂Sa(O1)

Sa(O2|O1) ⊂Sa(O2)

Definition 64 (Global Observable). The observable a ∈X(Oo) is called global if
for every pair of laboratory-type regions O1, O2 with Oo ⊂O1 ⊂O2 and O1 ̸= O2, it

Sa(O2|O1) ̸= Sa(O2)
In other words, it is global if it can never be geometrically localized in any of the
regions O1 that include Oo.

As we discussed in section 1.2, everything that happens in the past light cone
generated by the laboratory set LB has no effect on the preparation of the parametric
state and on the experimental procedures carried out in the laboratory itself; they
will only influence the act of measurement during the time interval between the end
of the preparation and the measurement itself — influences that we have established
to be identical in all copies of our ensemble20.

LA laboratory, as in Figure 14.8, it is not influenced by having prepared and carried

Let us now consider the case of the preparable conjunction in the two laboratories car-
ried out simultaneously. As mentioned, the various preparations of the experiments
take place at the same time in the two laboratories, as shown in Figures 14.924. Since
in this case the preparations take place simultaneously in the two laboratories, it can-
not be ruled out that the two experimenters may not be able to take countermeasures
to control the disturbances due to the individual preparations and measurements car-
ried out in the two laboratories. The measurement of the observable a in laboratory
A could make some states no longer suitable for the measurement of the observable b
in laboratory B (and vice versa), which could be prepared individually in that labo-
ratory. For example, the preparation carried out simultaneously in A could put some
equipment in laboratory B offside and thus limit the preparations of any experiments
for the measurement of B.

Before proceeding with the discussion, we must make a banal but necessary remark:
In the laboratory we have the instruments that are needed to measure our observ-
ables, and by definition they must be present only in the laboratory and not outside
it. In fact, we can say that it is precisely the devices that determine the extent of the
laboratory, unlike our source S of the measurement, which is not necessarily contained
in the laboratory.
Thus, the devices are internal to the laboratory, considered as a single measuring
device, while the source S is identified by the laboratory reference system considered.
For example, if we need to measure the intensity of a certain wave frequency from a
sound source S in laboratories A and B (see Figure 14.10), we have that S is identified
by the coordinates with respect to the two references27, while the measuring devices
are located in the respective laboratories with their preparation times, time that the
two experimenters have available to prepare the measurement and which may not be
sufficient for the measurement of this frequency (and therefore for its identification in
the laboratory).
Be careful: in this example, the experimenter in A does not perform the same ex-
periment as that in B, even if the observable to be measured is the same, since the
sound source S does not have the same spatial position with respect to the reference
systems centered in the two laboratories (so we may not have the same situation in
the two laboratories).
We also observe that if a is a physical quantity measurable in both laboratories LA
and LB, then it is possible to measure it jointly in the two laboratories, obtaining in

Physically we can set up two laboratories at rest with respect to each other, with the
same geometric characteristics (therefore the possibility of having the same devices,
the same experimental procedures, etc.) but positioned in different places in space.
What can we say about their observables and system states?
Having the same devices/instruments in the two copy laboratories A and B does not
ensure that the same observables can be detected in the two laboratories. Let us
specify the issue better by focusing on the meaning of the newly introduced term
detect28:
Since the two laboratories are identical with the same preparation times, etc., this
means that for each device positioned in LA there corresponds an identical one po-
sitioned in LB with the same preparations, conditions, etc.
Mathematically, this
translates into stating that each state ωA ∈SA identified through the reference sys-
tem (KA, OA) corresponds to one and only one copy state ωB ∈SB identified through
the reference system (KB, OB), and vice versa.
Therefore, if a ∈XA, then we have that the set SB
a ̸= ∅29 and one could have

which means that a ⊂0.
For example, in Figure 14.10, if the sound source S does not fall into the past light cone
of laboratory B, then the laboratory, even having internal instruments for measuring
sound frequencies, will not detect them.
We are now ready to formalize the issue by introducing a new postulate relating to
the two distinct laboratories at rest with respect to each other.

respect to each other and let (KA, OA) and (KB, OB) be their respective laboratory
reference systems. If the regions LA, LB ⊂E are superimposable through a space-
time translation30, then the two systems (XA, SA) and (XB, SB) have the following
properties:

14.5
Laboratory Inclusions
In our model we consider the laboratory system as a single body, where it is possible
to prepare the laboratory by activating procedures and devices in sequential actions.
This fact must be taken into account when you want to embed the two laboratories
into a larger one. In fact, in the two laboratories LA and LB the actions undertaken
to implement the experimental procedures can be performed autonomously from each
other, which cannot happen when they are considered part of a larger laboratory Lo.
Therefore, let us assume that we have two laboratories LA and LB, at rest with respect

• Second case: Joint Inclusion.
Compared to the previous situation, the matter becomes more delicate, since what can
be done simultaneously in two different laboratories, and therefore with two distinct
experimenters, cannot necessarily be done in a single laboratory. In the preparable
conjunction in Lo, the preparation of the observables is carried out sequentially. We
can prepare in Lo jointly, first a and then b (or vice versa) in the order a < b, or for
the simultaneous measurement in the order a : b, a preparation which differs from the
experimental procedures of the separate case, assuming that such preparations are
experimentally feasible. In fact, we must remember once again that in Lo we have
only one experimenter who carries out all the experimental procedures.
Thus, the problem is the following: include the preparation procedures of the two
laboratories LA and LB into a larger laboratory.
Let us ask ourselves the following problem:
In order to include the two preparations of the two laboratories LA and LB, con-
stituted by the laboratory-type regions OA and OB, into a single preparation of the

Definition 68. [Amalgamated State] Let a ∈XA and b ∈XB be jointly observable
and separately preparable in the two laboratories in the respective states ωA ∈SA
and ωB ∈SB, and suppose that a and b are compatible in the laboratory-type region
Oo of Lo.

ωo = ωA ▷◁ωB
Let us see what happens when we jointly include two laboratories where the prepa-
rations of the observables take place jointly simultaneously, separately in the two lab-
oratories LA and LB, as shown in Figure 14.9 of section 14.3.
Can we include the two preparations in Lo jointly for simultaneous or subsequent
preparation?
The answer is negative, for the same previous arguments: what can be done in two
distinct laboratories cannot be done in a single laboratory that extends both labora-
tories; in Lo we must first prepare a and then b or vice versa, and not simultaneously.

14.5.1
Independent Systems
We adapt to our case the algebraic notion of statistical independence, a notion that
can be found in [104, 123], to physical systems associated with sublaboratories.
The definitions we will give, differently from the algebraic case, depend heavily on
the methods of preparing the experiments in the various laboratories36.
Definition 69 (Singular Independence). Two physical systems (XA, SA) and
(XB, SB) associated with the two laboratory-type regions OA and OB, respectively,
are said to be singularly independent if, for any laboratory-type region Oo of Lo which
singularly includes the two laboratories located at A and B, then for every observable
x ∈XA, y ∈XB and ωA ∈SA
x , ωB ∈SB
y , there exists a state ω ∈S(Oo) such that:
• ω is suitable for both x and y, i.e., ω ∈Sx(Oo) ∩Sy(Oo);
• P Lo(x ∈∆, τ)ω = P LA(x ∈∆, τ)ωA;
• P Lo(y ∈∆, τ)ω = P LB(y ∈∆, τ)ωB.
We have a second definition of independence:
Definition 70. [Joint or Operational Independence] Two physical systems
(XA, SA) and (XB, SB) associated with the two laboratory-type regions OA and OB,
respectively, are said to be operationally independent if, for any laboratory-type region
Oo of Lo which jointly includes the two laboratories located at A and B, then for every
observables x ∈XA, y ∈XB that are compatible observables of the system (X, S)
related to Oo, and for states ωA ∈SA
x , ωB ∈SB
y , there exists a state ω ∈Sa:b(Oo)
such that:
P Lo(x ∈∆0 : y ∈∆1, τ)ω = P LA(x ∈∆0, τ)ωA P LB(y ∈∆1, τ)ωB
Remark 78. If the laboratory systems A and B are operationally independent, then
for every pair of observables x ∈XA and y ∈XB that are compatible in (X, S), they
are also singularly independent.
Indeed, from operational independence we obtain:
P Lo(x ∈∆0 : y ∈R, τ)ω = P LA(x ∈∆0, τ)ωA
and by compatibility,
P Lo(x ∈∆0 : y ∈R, τ)ω = P Lo(x ∈∆0, τ)ω
It follows that, for each τ ≥0, we obtain
P Lo(x ∈∆0, τ)ω = P LA(x ∈∆0, τ)ωA

Warning 16. In this case, it is not certain that the two physical systems (XA, SA)
and (XB, SB) corresponding to our two laboratories LA and LB are also operationally
independent.
In fact, having a state ωA in LA and ωB in LB means having established experimental
procedures and, with them, the various measuring instruments in these two laborato-
ries. To achieve operational independence of the two systems, it will be necessary
to identify a common state ω in Lo that simultaneously carries out the experimental
procedures contained in ωA and ωB, using the respective measurement instruments,
which is not always experimentally feasible.
14.6
Laboratories on the Move
As mentioned in the introduction, here we will not deal with relativistic issues in
detail; we will only address the meaning of invariance for laboratory-type regions.
We will extend Axiom 17 to the case where the two laboratories LA and LB are not
at rest with respect to each other, and their mutual motion is regulated by an element
of a group of transformations G, an element that establishes the change of coordinates
between the two reference systems (KA, OA) and (KB, OB), centered respectively in
our two laboratories as established in equation (13.8).
We will always assume the possibility that the two laboratories are physically capable
of transmitting information about the instruments adopted40, of sending each other
the various experimental protocols to be used in their respective laboratories for the
40Obviously, the time delay of communications due to the finite speed of any signals used must be
taken into account.

How do we determine when two laboratories are physically equivalent?

Definition 71. Two laboratory-type regions OA and OB are said to be G-equivalent

Question 12. Verify that this definition is mathematically well posed, i.e., that it is

The solution to our initial question is obtained by re-adapting Axiom 17 in an
obvious way. In fact, it is sufficient to replace the space-time translation with the
definition of G-equivalence. In other words, if OA and OB are G-equivalent, then the
associated physical systems (XA, SA) and (XB, SB) possess the properties listed in
Axiom 17.

These considerations are always experimentally true if we prepare the two states
ωA and ωB for a single measurement in the two laboratories. However, in EPR-type
experiments we are dealing with simultaneous measurements in both laboratories;
therefore we must arrange everything for a joint preparation.
As we saw in Sec-
tion 14.3, this is not always possible45. Thus we must assume that the preparation of
the two states can be done jointly, i.e., using the definition such that
ωA ∈SA▷◁B
a▷◁b (LA),
ωB ∈SA▷◁B
b▷◁a (LB)
In this way we obtain that
P LA(a ∈∆, 0)ωA = P LB(b ∈∆, 0)ωB
so the two observers will obtain the same values simultaneously (i.e., at the same time
τ = 0 as shown by both clocks at OA and OB), because both of their measurement
states have a common ancestor: they derive from the mother state ω ∈Sa:b(Lo).
This situation is operationally indistinguishable from the classical Regge ball ex-
ample: two boxes, one containing a white ball and the other a black ball, placed in
two separate laboratories. The correlation does not require any action at a distance;
it is inherited from the initial preparation.
Hence, within our model, the EPR “paradox” does not arise. What is sometimes
perceived as a quantum mystery is, in our framework, no more paradoxical than a
classical correlation established by a common preparation.
45This is possible precisely because the two laboratories are far apart and therefore do not perturb
each other during the joint preparation.

Part IV
Algebraic Interpretation
283


## Page 299


Chapter 15
The Algebra of Observables
... for a general observable, the choice of operator is as much of an art as a science,
since none of the "rules of quantization" known is of universal validity.
— Muga et al. [89]
In the previous sections we have not clearly specified the mathematical structure of
the sets of states S and observables X, so as to reveal only the basic framework of the
theory. But this freedom of action is only apparent, since the main physical theories,
such as classical mechanics and elementary quantum mechanics, require a less Spartan
mathematical structure than the one exposed so far, equipped with a more elaborate
mathematical formalism that has an effective exploratory function in understanding
the various physical phenomena that occur in nature.
We believe that the most suitable mathematical structure for this project is given
by the algebraization of the set of observables, where the set X is embedded, in an
appropriate way, in an associative algebra, and its set of states is seen as a subset of
its dual.
We will see that we do not have a general rule for establishing an algebraization
for a laboratory physical system, except in the particular case of classical or purely
quantum systems given by the quantization of phase space, which historically takes
the name of Weyl quantization1.
15.1
Algebraization of a Physical System
We will consider a mathematical model in which the set of observables is identified
with the self-adjoint elements of a unital C*-algebra A, which we will generically refer
to as the algebra of observables. Meanwhile, the set of physical states will be identified
with a subset of the set of states S(A) of the algebra A.
1This topic will not be discussed in these notes, since there are many texts in the literature where
it is exposed in an exhaustive way; e.g., for a rigorous mathematical treatment, we recommend
Folland’s book [53].
285


## Page 300


286
CHAPTER 15. THE ALGEBRA OF OBSERVABLES
It should be noted that the existence of this algebra associated with the entire physical
system of the laboratory is generally not guaranteed. To increase the likelihood of
success, we must instead consider the physical subsystems of our laboratory. In fact,
as we have seen, these subsystems are what hold real physical meaning, making the
possibility of algebraization more likely to materialize.
15.1.1
Representations on Associative Algebras
An algebraic representation (briefly, an algebraization) of the physical system (X, S)
is a triple (B, J, J♮) consisting of a unital real Banach algebra B and two maps
J : X →B

J(ra) = rJ(a)
(15.3)

For every observable a ∈X and state ω ∈Sa,
⟨a⟩ω = J♮(ω)

15.1.4
Spectral Connection
We now want to study the connection between the spectrum of the observable a and
the spectrum of J(a) in the real Banach algebra B. Fundamental to this analysis is
the embedding property introduced above. Of course, to have a good algebraization
of the physical system, these two spectra should coincide.
As is known, discussing the spectrum of the real Banach algebra B requires its com-
plexification:
BC = B ⊕jB
Indeed, if A is an element of the algebra B, the set of λ ∈R such that A −λI is
non-invertible in B could be empty8.
Recall that each element A ∈BC is written as
A = B1 + jB2 ,
B1, B2 ∈B
and there is a natural way to define an involution ∗: BC →BC:
(B1 + jB2)∗= B1 + j(−B2) ,
B1, B2 ∈B
(15.13)
Obviously, the self-adjoint elements of BC coincide with B9.
8See Bingren [18], p. 7, and Kaniuth [73] for further details on real operator algebras.

*-algebra BC a C*-algebra with the following properties:
a. ∥B + j0∥• = ∥B∥,
∀B ∈B
b. ∥B1 −jB2∥• = ∥B1 + B2∥• ,
∀B1, B2 ∈B
This topic is already covered in the first chapter of Li Bingren’s book [18], to which the interested
reader is referred.

The spectrum of an element A ∈B is defined as follows:
Sp(A) = {λ ∈C : λI −A is not invertible in BC}
It can be proven that the spectrum is non-empty and compact (cf. Kaniuth [73],
Proposition 1.2.8).10.
To determine the relationship between the spectrum σ(a) of the observable and the
spectrum Sp(J(a)) of its algebraization, we will use functional calculus as an inves-
tigative tool, applying it to both observables and associative algebras.
Let us make some observations about spectral mapping, starting with a simple
consequence of the properties of the map J, as follows:
Remark 80. For every real polynomial P, we have:
J(P(a)) = P(J(a)) ,
∀a ∈X
Now, let f : R →R be a continuous function. Since f(a) ∈X, it follows that
J(f(a)) ∈B. However, we cannot yet assert that
J(f(a)) = f(J(a))
(15.14)
because B has too weak an algebraic-topological structure to support continuous
functional calculus. As previously noted, functional calculus is a useful tool for es-
tablishing an explicit connection between the spectrum of an observable a and the
spectrum of J(a) as an element of the algebra.
This is one of the key motivations for enriching the mathematical structure of our
algebraization of a physical system — a topic we will explore in the next section.
Now we need to make a useful clarification:
Remark 81 (Associative real algebras vs.
complex algebras). Let A be a
generic complex *-algebra. The set of its self-adjoint elements, denoted by As.a., is
not generally a true associative algebra. However, with the Jordan product given in
(9.15), it becomes a real Jordan algebra (and thus a JC-algebra).
The next sections will study algebraizations of self-adjoint elements in complex C*-
algebras, which include the case of algebraizations on real Banach algebras.
Indeed, given a real Banach algebra B, the self-adjoint elements of its complexifica-
tion BC coincide with B, which by initial hypothesis is a real associative algebra,
unlike the set As.a..
Of course, we can further generalize the algebraizations over possible associative alge-
bras by considering the real C*-algebras AR11. We will not consider real associative

## Page 318

CHAPTER 15. THE ALGEBRA OF OBSERVABLES
Remark 84 (von Neumann algebra). To have a good algebraization, the C*-
algebra A must be closed in the weak topology; in other words, it must be a von
Neumann algebra. C*-algebras, unlike von Neumann algebras, are too poor in orthog-
onal projectors to contain the ’questions’ of the physical system29, and the projection
lattice P(A) corresponds to idealized measurement questions.
While we have established that the C*-algebra A should be weakly closed (mak-
ing it a von Neumann algebra), we must still determine which specific von Neumann
algebra most appropriately contains all observables of the physical system.
Furthermore, we must characterize the image of the state map J♮: S →S(A) un-
der our algebraization, particularly whether it consists entirely of normal states in A∗.
Given these considerations regarding von Neumann algebras and normal states,
Kastler’s seminal observation provides crucial physical insight [74]:
Quote 4. It is important to realize that whilst the quasi-local algebra A has ele-
ments corresponding to physical observables (procedures), this is no longer the case
for the weak closure M = π(A)′′ in some representation π. It is not correct, from
a physical point of view, to consider global quantities (such as the bounded functions
of the energy, or the number operator), as observables, although they belong to M
for certain representations. Indeed these cannot be observed locally, and only local
experiments are physically possible. Thus the W*-systems30 obtained by consid-
ering the weak closure in covariant representations together with the extended group
action should not be considered as physical systems, but as certain mathe-
matical extensions of physical systems pertaining to certain particular physical
situations (physical states). This point is important for a correct realization of the
respective roles of C* and W* algebras in algebraic field theory.
The topic will be returned to next chapter.

I. Choosing the Hilbert Space H.
Since the sets of observables and states are countable, we select a separable

Because H is separable, its dimension is either finite or countably infinite. More-
