## Page 321


Chapter 16
Physical vs. Mathematical
States
In this section, we resume the analysis begun in the previous chapter 7, with the aim
of clarifying in a more structural way the relationship between physical states and
algebraic states in the representation induced by J♮.
We will see that there is a distinction between mathematical eigenstates and physical
eigenstates: mathematical eigenstates, i.e., vectors in a Hilbert space, do not always
correspond to pure physical states of the system. Even if a vector Ψh is an eigen-
vector of the operator J(a), it is not guaranteed that there exists a physical state ωh
representing it in a pure way, that is, satisfying J♮(ωh)(A) = ⟨Ψh|AΨh⟩.
This observation has implications for the notion of mixed states understood as sta-
tistical mixtures. In other words, a physical state ω satisfying P(a = λ)ω = 1, and
thus being an eigenstate of the observable a, can still be a mixed state in the alge-
braic representation. Its representation J♮(ω) can indeed be a statistical mixture of
mathematical pure states, each of which is an eigenvector of J(a).
Therefore, the image of physical eigenstates under the map J♮is not necessarily con-
tained in the pure states of A. This means that physical eigenstates do not always
correspond to pure states in the algebraic representation, and we can affirm that there
exists a gap between the mathematical description, based on eigenvectors in H, and
the actual physical states of the system.

In quantum mechanics, the Hilbert space is often separable, and the density matrix

Its eigenvalues {λi} form a non-negative summable sequence with P

This inequality holds for both finite and infinite-dimensional spaces.
Thus, the density matrix ρ is a Hilbert–Schmidt operator, i.e., ρ ∈L2(H), and its

2 = tr(ρ2) ≤1
It is straightforward to verify that

It follows that if tr(ρ2) < 1, the state ρ is mixed.
In other words, this norm measures purity:
∥ρ∥2 = 1 for pure states, and ∥ρ∥2 < 1 for mixed states.
Let us examine the relationship between purity and von Neumann entropy:
- The purity p(ρ) is a quadratic measure of mixedness: it is 1 for pure states and
decreases for mixed states.
- The von Neumann entropy S(ρ) = −tr(ρ log ρ) is an entropic measure: it is 0
for pure states and increases for mixed states.
Both quantities measure how "far" a state is from being pure. There is no direct,
universal algebraic relationship between p(ρ) and S(ρ) because they depend on the
eigenvalues of ρ in different ways.
However, they are both expressed in terms of the eigenvalues λk of ρ:
• p(ρ) = P
k λ2
k,
• S(ρ) = −P
k λk log λk.

16.2.3
Purity for a Physical State
We define the notion of a purity index for a physical state ω ∈Sk
a of the laboratory
system. To do this, we must consider, as in the case of von Neumann entropy, the set
of operationally realizable density matrices Dk
ω,a defined on page 147:
p(ω, a) = inf

tr(ρ2): ρ ∈Dk
ω,a
	
The index p(ω, a) ∈(0, 1] is called the purity degree of the state ω with respect to the
measurement of the observable a.
p(ω, a) = 1 ⇐⇒Dk
ω,a is indecomposable
⇐⇒Sk(ω, a) = 0 ⇐⇒ω ∈Ext(Sk
a)
The purity of a state ω, to be a good quality index of the state itself, must be evaluated
on every observable suitable for it; in other words, on every observable x ∈Xω.
To obtain a good purity index for the state, one should average the value p(ω, x) over
the whole set Xω, which is experimentally unfeasible.
We choose another path: we assume as the purity degree of ω its most “mixed” value:
p(ω) = inf {p(ω, x): x ∈Xω}
In this way, if p(ω) = 1, then p(ω, x) = 1 for every x ∈Xω. This serves as a robust
operational definition of a pure state. Conversely, if p(ω) < 1, the state is mixed.
◦◦◦
We now examine the problem posed in Question 15 on page 290:
Does the C*-algebraization (A, J, J♮) preserve purity?
But what does it mean for an algebraization to preserve purity?
Let us give a possible answer:
Purity preservation ensures that the algebraic states J♮(ω) retain the physical inter-
pretability of ω as an "extremal preparation".
In physics, pure states represent the most precise possible preparation of a system.
If the map J♮preserves purity, then a perfectly prepared state in the physical lab-
oratory system remains a perfectly prepared state in the algebraic representation.
Without purity preservation, an extremal state in the physical laboratory system
might become mixed (non-extremal) in the C*-algebraization (A, J, J♮), losing its in-
terpretation as a "sharp" preparation. But is this question well-posed?
Thus, the following problem must be solved:
Given a state ω ∈Sk
a, determine the density matrix ρω ∈B(H) that implements the
algebraic state J♮(ω), i.e.,
J♮(ω)(B) = tr(ρωB),
∀B ∈A
such that the following equation holds:
p(ρω) = p(ω)


## Page 332


318
CHAPTER 16. PHYSICAL VS. MATHEMATICAL STATES
In other words, given p(ω) ∈(0, 1], determine ρ ∈B(H) such that
tr(ρ2) = p(ω).
(16.6)
Note that if (16.6) admits a solution when p(ω) = 1, then the associated state J♮(ω)
is pure.
We will see later13 that this equation does not always admit solutions, and that purity
cannot always be trivially preserved under algebraization.

Proof. Assume φ is not pure. Then there exist distinct states φ1, φ2 ∈S(A) and

Let A = B −φ(B)I. Then:
φ(A) = φ(B) −φ(B) = 0
For any state γ, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives |γ(A2)| ≥γ(A)2.

Since φ1(A) = φ1(B) −φ(B) ̸= φ2(B) −φ(B) = φ2(A) (therefore φ1(A)2 ̸= φ2(A)2),
the inequality is strict:
φ(A2) > 0
But φ(A) = 0, so ∆φ(A) = φ(A2) > 0, contradicting the hypothesis. Thus, φ must
be pure.
In other words, the previous proposition states that if νφ,A ∈Πp for every A ∈
As.a., then φ is a pure state.

Spectral Digression
This section is intended for readers familiar with the Hilbert space formalism. We
briefly recall some properties of self-adjoint operators and their spectral projections, in
order to compare them with our notion of observables and spectral projections 1∆(a).
The aim is to highlight similarities and differences between the two approaches, and
to prepare the ground for the algebraic formulation.
Let A be a self-adjoint and unitary operator on a Hilbert space H. We study the
operator 1{λ}(A), the spectral projection associated with λ.

Conversely, if λ ∈Sp(A), it may still happen that 1{λ}(A) = 0.
If 1{λ}(A) ̸= 0, we have:
1{λ}(A)H = ker(λI −A)

The answer to this question is not at all straightforward. In the literature, similar

The second question is more difficult to resolve. One should verify that there exists
at least one limit point satisfying these two conditions, a task we will not undertake
here. In fact, as we have seen, purity is not always preserved in the transition to
the algebraic framework.
Consequently, we are forced to make a mathematically
convenient choice: to select the density matrix with the lowest possible purity index
(even if it does not match the true purity of our physical state) or, alternatively, the
one with the highest von Neumann entropy. In both cases, this leads to a discrepancy
with physical reality.
16.4
Spectrum of Observables and C*-algebraization
We will analyze the spectral types of observables and self-adjoint operators, along
with their comparison.
We have established that for a good algebraization, the spectral relation
σ(a) = Sp(J(a))
must remain preserved for every observable a ∈X. However, this relation alone is not
yet sufficient to fully characterize our algebraization.
Let a be a non null observable of the system.
On page 117, we defined the set
σpd(a) as the set of isolated points of the spectrum of a and established the following

that Uλ ∩σ(a) ̸= ∅26.
Furthermore, we recall the implication:

=⇒
λ ∈σ(a)
whose converse is not true in general:
If λ ∈σ(a), it does not necessarily follow that 1{λ}(a) ̸= 0
We now define the following new subsets of the spectrum:
• Pure point spectrum:
σpp(a) =

λ ∈σ(a) : 1{λ}(a) ̸= 0
	
• Continuous spectrum:
σc(a) =

λ ∈σ(a) : 1{λ}(a) = 0
	
Consequently, the spectrum decomposes into the following disjoint union:
σ(a) = σpp(a) ∪σc(a)

16.5
Equivalent Algebraic Representations
Experimental information is intrinsically contained in the pair (X, S), which includes,
for example, the spectrum of every observable of the system and its type. Therefore,
the possible algebraic representations of our physical system must "reproduce" this
information faithfully, making it mathematically more usable.
For an algebrization (A, J, J♮) to be a good representation, it must necessarily preserve
the spectrum of each observable and its type unchanged, as established by Proposition
59:
σpp(a) = Spp(J(a))
,
σc(a) = Spc(J(a))
As for the dual representation J♮, it should preserve as much as possible the degree
of purity of the physical states, as discussed in the previous sections of this chapter.
Two algebrizations (A0, J0, J♮
0) and (A1, J1, J♮
1) of the same physical system (X, S),
to be considered equivalent, must satisfy for every observable a ∈X the following
spectral property:
Spess(J0(a)) = Spess(J1(a)),
Spdis(J0(a)) = Spdis(J1(a))
(16.21)
Furthermore, for every ω ∈Sa it must:
• keep the purity of the algebraic states invariant:
p(ρ0) = p(ρ1)
• and keep their entropy invariant:
S(ρ0) = S(ρ1)

Theorem 11. If Φ is an affine mapping of the family Sσ(A) of weakly-continuous
states of a von Neumann algebra A acting on the Hilbert space H into the correspond-

eω(α(B)) = ]

Observe that if Φ is a bijective affine isomorphism between the state spaces of
C*-algebras or von Neumann algebras, then it automatically preserves pure states,
because pure states are exactly the extreme points of the compact convex set S(A),
and a bijective affine isomorphism of convex sets maps extreme points to extreme
points.

Chapter 17
Algebraizations and
Compatibility
In this section, we study the problem of algebraically representing the experimental
measurements of two or more compatible observables of the system when they are
measured simultaneously.
We will see that, in order to identify the experimental
measurement µω,a:b obtained from the experimental frequencies with a theoretical
joint spectral measure µφ,A:B, the self-adjoint operators A, B associated with a, b
respectively via algebraization must necessarily commute.

## Page 360


346
CHAPTER 17. ALGEBRAIZATIONS AND COMPATIBILITY
17.2
Final discussion
Let J : X →A be the map assigning to each experimental observable a a self-adjoint
operator J(a) in the algebra A. As we have seen, to identify the experimental joint
measure µω,a:b with the theoretical joint spectral measure µˆω,J(a):J(b), it must hold
that:
µω,a:b = µˆω,J(a):J(b)
This identification is possible only if the joint spectral measure on the right-hand side
exists, which requires that J(a) and J(b) commute. Hence, the map J must preserve
compatibility in the following sense:
a, b compatible (experimentally simultaneously measurable) ⇒[J(a), J(b)] = 0
If this condition fails, the joint spectral measure µˆω,J(a):J(b) is not defined within the
usual spectral theory, and the equality above loses its meaning.
We emphasize that the commutativity of J(a) and J(b) is a mathematical property
of the representation, whereas experimental compatibility is a physical fact concerning
the existence of an apparatus or procedure for simultaneous measurement.
Remark 90. If a and b are experimentally compatible observables, then in the theory
they must be represented by commuting operators:
[J(a), J(b)] = 0.
The converse is not automatic, nor is it a general property. Commutativity alone does
not guarantee that two observables can be measured simultaneously in the laboratory;
it merely ensures that, within the mathematical model, a joint spectral measure exists.
We are convinced that many famous paradoxes of quantum mechanics arise pre-
cisely from confusing:
• experimental compatibility (the existence of a simultaneous measurement pro-
cedure);
• mathematical commutativity (the vanishing of the commutator [J(a), J(b)]),
and from forgetting that the map J must preserve compatibility, but that commuta-
tivity alone is not sufficient to determine whether two observables are compatible in
the physical world.
In other words, in a rigorous treatment of quantum mechanics, one should always
state:
• that self-adjoint operators are not the observables, but represent observables via
a map J;
• that experimental compatibility implies [J(a), J(b)] = 0;


## Page 361


17.2. FINAL DISCUSSION
347
• that [J(a), J(b)] = 0 does not automatically imply that a and b are compatible.
Recognizing this distinction avoids many misunderstandings and restores conceptual
clarity to the structure of quantum theory.
Another problem that arises in various cases is the following statement:
If you measure A and then B, the result is the same as measuring B and then A if A
and B commute.
This is true, but there is a misinterpretation that is sometimes added:
namely, that commuting A and B can nevertheless be seen as one measured before
the other in an operational temporal sense, and that the product AB corresponds to
first A and then B.
But this is not the case:
The operator product AB (in our case J(a)J(b)) does not physically represent mea-
suring A and then B in a sequential sense, even if they commute; rather, it represents
the product of the two operators in the algebraic sense, which is well-defined only
because they commute.


## Page 362


348
CHAPTER 17. ALGEBRAIZATIONS AND COMPATIBILITY


## Page 363


Chapter 18
Some Algebraic Questions
This chapter collects several algebraic issues that arise naturally once a C* - alge-
braization (A, J, J♮) of a physical system has been introduced.
We examine how
such an algebraization accommodates the description of time evolution (Schrödinger
and Heisenberg pictures), the compatibility of algebraizations with nested laboratory
regions (isotony), the lifting of geometric symmetries to algebraic symmetries, and
finally a tentative algebraic model for sequential measurements via free algebras.
Each of these topics touches upon foundational questions at the interface between
physics and operator algebras. The discussion is intentionally kept at a rather ex-
ploratory level; many of the constructions and definitions proposed here are only
sketched, and their deeper implications, such as the precise conditions for isotony, the
existence of algebraic time maps, the classification of algebraic symmetries, or the role
of free algebras in non-commutative probability, are left for future investigation. This
chapter therefore serves as a roadmap for further research, highlighting directions that
deserve a more systematic treatment.

## Page 369


Chapter 19
Algebras and State Selections
In this section, we will analyze the algebraic realizations (A, J, J♮) of physical subsys-
tems (Xo, So) of the laboratory physical system Lo.
We will study the role played by the center of its observables ZSo(Xo) in establishing
the possible algebraic realizations of a physical system. To this end, we will need to
introduce additional properties that the maps J and J♮must satisfy to obtain a phys-
ically suitable mathematical description of our laboratory system; in other words, we
must specify to which selection rules they must adhere.
19.1
Jordan and Segal Algebraic Realizations
The C*-algebraic approach we introduced is not the most general possible algebraic
realization for a physical subsystem (Xo, So).
Let us therefore consider an algebraic realization (B, J, J♮) where (B, ◦) is a real JB-
algebra of Jordan and J : Xo →B and J♮: So →S(B)1 that always satisfy the ARBA
conditions given in Section 15.1 on page 285, along with the embedding property (12).
We emphasize that a JB-algebra is formally real (cf. [58], Corollary 3.3.8)2. Obvi-
ously, the C*-algebraic case treated in the previous section is a special case of Jordan
algebraic realizations, precisely those obtained through JW-algebras.
If a, b ∈Xo are So-compatible observables of the system, by the definition of the
Jordan product given in (9.10) on page 178 and from the properties of the ARBA con-
ditions of the Jordan algebraic realization, it is easy to verify that in this case too,
relation (15.9) is satisfied.
As we have previously verified, if {a, b, c} are compatible observables, we have a·(b·c) =
(a·b)·c, and also in this case, we obtain that relation (15.10) on page 290 is satisfied.

Recall that (Z(B), ◦) defined in equation (9.14) on page 181 turns out to be an asso-

This leads to the following definition:
Definition 77. [Central Condition] The algebraic realization (B, J, J♮) of the phys-
ical system (Xo, So) satisfies the central condition if
J(ZSo(Xo)) ⊂Z(B)
(19.1)
Remark 91. Relation (15.9)

does not guarantee the central condition.
We note that from relation (9.13), the central condition is satisfied if and only if
TJ(a)TB = TBTJ(a) for all elements a ∈ZSo(Xo) and B ∈B; therefore, if and only if
(B ◦X) ◦J(a) = B ◦(X ◦J(a)) ,
∀B, X ∈B, a ∈ZSo(Xo).
We have the following question:
Why not directly use the more general real JB-algebras for the algebraic realization
of a physical system, instead of resorting to the C*-algebraic approach?
An attempt to answer our question was provided by Horuzhy in [63], p. 10:
Quote 5. Such noncanonical schemes have not been developed very far, however, as
they involve a lot of mathematical difficulties which are not balanced by noticeable
physical gains.
The mathematical difficulties mentioned in the previous statement lie in the fact
that for generic JB-algebras, we have a spectral analysis that is more difficult to han-
dle and a functional calculus that is, in practice, restricted to holomorphic functions
(cf. [14, 58]), whereas this is not the case for JW-algebras. Moreover, for the latter,
we have a connection, given by Topping’s Proposition 29 on page 183, between the
center of the Jordan algebra and its commutant.
Historically, after the start of the joint work of Jordan, von Neumann, and Wigner
in 1934 [69], applications of Jordan algebras to physics came to a halt. They were
later proposed in another form by Segal in his postulates for quantum mechanics [114],
through the definition of what are now called Segal systems3.
A Segal system consists of a real Banach space Q and the existence, for each
natural number n, of a continuous map in norm, called the n-th power,
A ∈Q −→An ∈Q
such that for every A, B ∈Q we have:
3Segal’s postulates were refined by Sherman in [116].

• ∥A2 −B2∥≤max

• ∥A2∥= ∥A∥2.
Now it is easy to verify that the space Q with the formal product given in (9.10)
becomes a real Jordan algebra.

Central Connections
Let us abandon the more general Jordan algebraic realizations and analyze the conse-

realizations of our physical system.

19.3
Selection rules for algebraizations
Unlike our approach, where the sets of observables and states are initial data of
the model, in algebraic theory one starts from an operator algebra, often concrete,
where observables are identified with its self-adjoint elements. This identification is
generally not surjective, since not all self-adjoint elements of the algebra are physically
relevant. Hence the introduction of selection mechanisms on the algebras that attempt
to delimit such observables within the algebra, historically known as superselection
rules:
Quote 6. In its most general form, a superselection rule for a quantum mechanical
theory can be defined as any restriction on what is observable in the theory10.
As already stated, in our model the physical quantities and the states of the sys-
tem are given. The problem is therefore to determine an appropriate algebraization
that adequately describes the physical phenomenon under consideration. We have
already briefly indicated in section 15.3 the strategy to follow for this purpose. Here
we intend to introduce additional properties that algebraizations ought to satisfy in
order to better describe a physical subsystem of our quantum laboratory.
Our starting point is the following:
Let a C*-algebraization (A, J, J♮) be given, with A a concrete C*-algebra on a Hilbert
space H which is weakly closed, of the physical subsystem (Xo, So) satisfying the
central condition.
Assume furthermore that ZSo(Xo) ̸= ∅.
By Proposition 62, for every z ∈ZSo(Xo) we have
J(a)J(z) = J(z)J(a) ,
∀a ∈Xo
hence
J(ZSo(Xo)) ⊂J(Xo)′
(19.10)
and relation (19.8) follows.
We have a simple statement about the non-trivial center:
10Citation from Strocchi and Wightman [122].

Proposition 63.

If A ∈J(ZSo(Xo)), by hypothesis A = rI for some real r.
Thus J(Cr(a)) = J(A) where the observable Cr(a) ⊂rI with Cr(t) = r for every
t ∈R.
The observables Cr(a) and a are compatible and therefore we can write J(a−Cr(a)) =
0 and by the embedding property a −Cr(a) ⊂0.
Definition 79. [Selection Rules] A C*-algebraization (A, J, J♮) of the physical sys-
tem (Xo, So) possesses selection rules (SR) if the algebra J(Xo)′ ⊂B(H) is non-
trivial, i.e.,
J(Xo)′ ̸= CI

A = B(H) ⇐⇒no selection rules are present

Hence the algebra A = Z(A) is abelian.

Now it is useful to recall two well-known facts from operator algebra theory:
Proposition 64. [Theorem 9.1.3 of [71]] If a von Neumann algebra M is of type I
(resp. type II, type III), then the same holds for its commutant M′.
Proposition 65. [Problem 9.6.1 of [71]] A von Neumann algebra M is of type I if
and only if M is ∗-isomorphic to a von Neumann algebra with abelian commutant.
From these two important statements, the following result follows:
Proposition 66. If our algebraization admits a complete system of compatible ob-
servables, then the algebra A is of type I.
Proof. Assume that X⊘is a complete system of compatible observables. By definition,
R = J(X⊘)′′ and R′ = J(X⊘)′, hence
R ⊂A
=⇒
A′ ⊂R′ = R ⊂A.
Thus A′ is a subalgebra of an abelian algebra, and therefore it is itself abelian. By
Proposition 65, we obtain that A′′ = A is of type I (and consequently, by Proposition
64, A′ is also of type I).

A ⊂J(ZSo(Xo))′ ⊂B(H)

Property 19 (StSR). The physical subsystem (Xo, So) admits a C*-algebraization

B(H)s.a. ∩J(ZSo(Xo))′ ⊂As.a.

J(ZSo(Xo))′ ⊂A
and from (19.7) and minimality:
A′′ = J(Xo)′′ ⊂J(ZSo(Xo))′

19.5
Algebrization for Mackey Systems*
We resume the discussion on algebrizations by applying it to Mackey systems, as de-
fined in §12.5.
Let (Xo, So) be a Mackey system and (A, J, J♮) a C*-algebrization of it. We assume
that it satisfies the embedding property and the state separation property as estab-
lished in the previous sections.
In particular, the state separation property takes the following form for Mackey sys-
tems:
Property 20 (PS). The algebrization (A, J, J♮) of our Mackey system satisfies the
state separation property if for every observable a ∈Xo it holds:

A ∈As.a. such that J♮(ω)(A) = 0 ∀ω ∈So

=⇒
A = 0
(19.16)
In addition to the properties listed in Section 15.1, new properties arise for Mackey
systems. For example, from the previous sections we derived relation (15.7), which
does not establish the actual convexity of the map J♮but suggests it.
To this end, we can introduce the following further condition for Mackey systems:

We also extend the continuity condition to the weak topologies:
II. The map J♮: So →S(A) is weakly continuous:

From the affine property we obtain that the subset of states J♮(So) ⊂S(A) is convex.
From the injectivity of the map J♮19 we can assert that if ω ∈So is a pure state of
the system, then J♮(ω) is a pure algebraic state of the set J♮(So).

In conclusion, the existence — in a non operational sense — of a canonical evolu-
tion in the sense of Mackey cannot be considered a simple assumption of the model.
It is realized only for very restricted classes of physical systems, namely those for
which it is possible to construct a C*-algebrization endowed with particularly strong
structural properties, given by assumptions [A1], [A2], [A3].
25See also [23], Proposition 3.2.8

## Page 389


Final Remarks
Talking once about a colleague who had spent a few years in Germany, Wigner said:
“... and when he came back he was transformed into a German physicist.” Puzzled, I
asked: “What is a German physicist?” The answer: “Well, an American physicist, if
he has no ideas, makes himself useful — perhaps he calculates something. A
German physicist, if he has no ideas, simply does nothing.”
Rudolf Haag — Personal discussion with Wigner, 1957–59 [41].
In these notes, I have chosen to return to the laboratory itself: to the measurement
instruments, the preparation protocols, and the operations that define what can be
done and what can be observed. From this operational context, I have attempted
to reconstruct the quantum formalism - not by postulating a Hilbert space or an
algebraic structure from the outset. Only in the final section did we explore the pos-
sibility of embedding a physical system into an operator algebra, while emphasizing
the distinction between the mathematical framework and the operationalist perspec-
tive adopted here.
A natural continuation of these notes would be to analyze in greater detail the re-
lation between the physical subsystems of a laboratory (as discussed in the previous
sections) and their possible algebrizations - a topic closely connected to the notion of
independence of local algebras in the algebraic approach (cf. [123]).
These notes are not a closed system.
They are, rather, an invitation to rethink the foundations of quantum physics starting
from the only things we truly possess: a laboratory, some instruments, and the ability
to repeat experiments.
I am not a German physicist.
So I wrote a book.
Comments, suggestions, criticisms, and reports of errors are most
welcome.
